However, due to (
2.2
), we never get trees with internal nodes from the Hall triple set.

Summary
:
The basis
\mathcal{B}
for
\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})
consists of

•

Trees with internal nodes from
C
and leaves from the Hall triple set of trees.

•

The Hall triple set of trees.

4
Hall basis for free Lie triple system

Recall that an LTS is a vector space with a tri-linear bracket satisfying (
2.3
)–(
2.5
).
We construct a Hall basis for
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
, the free LTS over a set
\mathcal{C}
.
A classical result
[
14
,
15
]
states that an LTS can always be embedded in a Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,])
as a subspace
closed under the triple bracket
[a,b,c]:=[[a,b],c]
.
In particular,
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
can be embedded in
\operatorname{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
, the free Lie algebra
[
23
]
over
\mathcal{C}
.

\operatorname{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading where Lie monomials with an odd (resp. even) degree have grade
1
(resp.
0
). The odd monomials span
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
. In particular, any odd monomial can be rewritten as a sum of iterated triple brackets.
For example, using the Jacobi rule and skew symmetry of
[\,\_\,,\,\_\,]
, we find

[[[a,b],[c,d]],e]=[a,b,[c,d,e]]-[c,d,[a,b,e]].

Hence any Hall basis for
\operatorname{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
yields a basis for
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
, by restricting to the odd monomials. However, such bases will in general contain odd monomials which are not simple iterated triple brackets, but must be rewritten as sums of triple brackets.
For example, the Lyndon basis
[
23
]
for
\operatorname{Lie}(\{a,b,c,d,e\})
contains the odd monomial
[[[a,e],[c,d]],b]
.

To obtain a basis for
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
consisting of only triple brackets, we propose a Hall-type basis obtained directly on
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
, independent of an embedding in
\operatorname{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
. Let
(\operatorname{Mag}_{3}(\mathcal{C}),[\,\_\,,\,\_\,,\,\_\,])
denote the
triple magma
over
\mathcal{C}
, i.e., all possible iterated triple brackets formed from
\mathcal{C}
. This can be represented as planar ternary trees with leaves coloured by
\mathcal{C}
. Let
|\,\_\,|\colon\operatorname{Mag}_{3}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the grading
|c|=1
for
c\in\mathcal{C}
and
|[a,b,c]|=|a|+|b|+|c|
for general
a
,
b
,
c
. Let
\operatorname{Alg}_{3}(\mathcal{C})
denote the vector space spanned by
\operatorname{Mag}_{3}(\mathcal{C})
over some field
K
, and let
\mathcal{M}\subset\operatorname{Alg}_{3}(\mathcal{C})
be the ideal generated by the Lie triple relations (
2.3
)–(
2.5
).

Definition 4.1

(Hall triple set)
.

We define the Hall triple set
H
as

•

\mathcal{C}\subset H\subset\operatorname{Mag}_{3}(\mathcal{C})
.

•

H
has a total ordering
<
such that
|u|<|v|
implies
u<v
. The ordering on the same grade is arbitrary, e.g., a lexicographical rule.

•

[u,v,w]\in H
if and only if
u,v,w\in H
,
u>v\leq w
and one of the following hold:

–

either
u\in\mathcal{C}
,

–

or
u=[a,b,c]
where
c\leq v
.

Proposition 4.2
.

H
is a basis for the free Lie triple system
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

First, we show by induction in the grading that any monomial in
\operatorname{Alg}_{3}(\mathcal{C})
can be expressed as a sum of the Hall elements added with terms contained in
\mathcal{M}
.
We introduce a rewriting based on (
2.3
)–(
2.5
).
Given a monomial
[u,v,w]
. As induction hypothesis, we assume
u,v,w\in H
.

(1)

Using (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), rewrite
[u,v,w]
so that the smallest element is in the middle:

(a)

If
u=v
,
[u,v,w]\mapsto 0
.

(b)

If
u<v
,
[u,v,w]\mapsto-[v,u,w]
.

(c)

If
u>v>w
,
[u,v,w]\mapsto[u,w,v]-[v,w,u]
.

After this, we have obtained terms of the form
[u,v,w]
, where
u>v\leq w
. If
u\in\mathcal{C}
or
u=[a,b,c]
, where
c\leq v
this is Hall, and we stop.

(2)

For
[u,v,w]
, where
u>v\leq w
,
u=[a,b,c]
and
c>v
, rewrite using (
2.5
):

[[a,b,c],v,w]\mapsto[a,b,[c,v,w]]-[c,[a,b,v],w]-[c,v,[a,b,w]].

(4.1)

Repeat (1) and (2) on the three terms in (
4.1
) until all terms are Hall.

We need to show that this rewriting will always stop.

Consider the first term on the right in (
4.1
),
[a,b,[c,v,w]]
.
If
c\in\mathcal{C}
or if
c=[c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime\prime}]
with
c^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq v
then
[a,b,[c,v,w]]\in H
. To see this, recall we have assumed
u=[a,b,c]\in H
, so
a=[a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime\prime}]
with
a^{\prime\prime\prime}>b
is not possible. Furthermore,
b<a
and
b\leq c
implies
b<[c,v,w]
, and we have
c>v\leq w
, which shows that it is Hall. If
c=[c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime\prime}]
with
c>v
, we use the induction hypothesis to write
[c,v,w]
as a sum of
Hall elements
h_{i}\in H
,
h_{i}>b
. This yields Hall elements
[a,b,h_{i}]\in H
.

Now we continue to rewrite the two rightmost terms in (
4.1
). We will argue that the Hall elements

[a,b,h_{i}]\in H
produced from the first term cannot reappear in the rewriting of the last two terms. If this is so, each round of rewriting removes some Hall words completely from the remaining terms, and the iteration must eventually stop.

To see that
[a,b,h_{i}]\in H
will not reappear, we first consider the second term
[c,[a,b,v],w]
. Rewriting could give

[[a,b,v],c,w]
or
[[a,b,v],w,c]
. However, since
v<c
and
v\leq w
, we will never use (
4.1
) and get back terms of the form
[a,b,[\dots]]
.

The last term is
[c,v,[a,b,w]]
, where
v<c
and
v\leq w
, thus
v<[a,b,w]
. The smallest term is
v
in the middle, so we
will not use rewriting (c)
and get back terms with
a
,
b
moved to the left, hence
[a,b,[\dots]]
can not reappear.

This shows that
H
spans
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
. To see that
H
is a basis, we note that the rewriting rules guarantee that (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) hold for all
a,b,x,y,z\in H
. By tri-linearity, these relations must also hold in the span of
H
, and hence
H
is a basis for the quotient
\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Alg}_{3}(\mathcal{C})/\langle(\ref{eq:LTS1}),(\ref{eq:LTS2}),(\ref{eq:LTS3})\rangle
.
∎

Definition 4.3
.

Given a totally ordered set
\mathcal{C}
, let
\mathfrak{H}\colon\operatorname{Alg}_{3}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{Alg}_{3}(\mathcal{C})
be the linear projection that rewrites any element of
\operatorname{Mag}_{3}(\mathcal{C})
into a sum of Hall elements according to the algorithm described in the proof above, i.e., such that
\ker(\mathfrak{H})=\mathcal{M}
and
Im(\mathfrak{H})=\operatorname{LTS}(\mathcal{C})
.

For
\operatorname{LTS}(\{a,b\})
, the Hall triple set up to order 7 is

\displaystyle H=\{{}

\displaystyle a,b,[baa],[bab],[[baa]aa],[[baa]ab],[[baa]bb],[[bab]bb],[ba[baa]],[ba[bab]],[[[baa]aa]aa],

\displaystyle{}[[[baa]aa]ab],[[[baa]aa]bb],[[[baa]ab]bb],[[[baa]bb]bb],[[[bab]bb]bb],[ba[[baa]aa]],

\displaystyle{}[ba[[baa]ab]],[ba[[baa]bb]],[ba[[bab]bb]],[ba[ba[baa]]],[ba[ba[bab]]],[[baa]a[baa]],

\displaystyle{}[[baa]b[baa]],[[bab]b[baa]],[[baa]a[bab]],[[baa]b[bab]],[[bab]b[bab]],\dots\}.

The dimensions of the graded components,
2,2,6,18,56,186,\dots
are computed by the classical Witt formula for odd grades, see
A001037
.

5
OSBB-words: a basis for the free tensor algebra

In this section, consider a vector space
W
over a field
K
with a totally ordered basis
\mathcal{C}
, and let
\prec
denote the total order on
\mathcal{C}
. Let
T(W)
be the free tensor algebra over
W
, equipped with the structure and notation introduced in Section
3.1
.

Recall from Section
3.3
that one way to generate the planar rooted trees is to
graft
an
ordered forest
(or a word) onto a
root
. If instead this process is done by only grafting
unordered forests

\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{k})
onto a root, we would generate the non-planar rooted trees, which is a basis for the free pre-Lie algebra. This algebra is defined by the relation
[\,\_\,,\,\_\,,\,\_\,]=0
, hence the triple-brackets together with non-planar trees should in some sense be sufficient to describe the full free algebra
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
. The triple-bracket can be described by grafting a bracket
[x,y]
onto a tree;
[x,y]\rhd z=[x,y,z]
.

The motivation to continue this journey of combinatorics on the tensor algebra is to be able to generate a basis for the free algebra using triple-brackets and non-planar trees instead of planar trees, which corresponds to a basis on the free tensor algebra using symmetrized elements and brackets. This alternative basis on the tensor algebra is presented in Theorem
5.1
below, after we have developed some language.

•

Symmetric word:
Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
,
k\geq 0
be elements from the set
\mathcal{C}
. The element
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{k})
is called a
symmetric word
. Note that the empty word is a symmetric word.

•

Symmetric-bracket word:
If
y,z\in\mathcal{C}
, then the element
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{k})\cdot[y,z]
is called a
symmetric-bracket word
.

•

SBB-word
An element
\omega=\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{s}
is called a
symmetric-bracket-block word
, or an SBB-word, if
\omega_{i}
is a symmetric-bracket word for
1\leq i<s
and
\omega_{s}
is a symmetric word. As long as
\omega_{s}
is non-empty, we say that
\omega
has
s

blocks
, where
\omega_{i}
is a block of
\omega
for
1\leq i\leq s
. If
\omega_{s}
is the empty word,
\omega
has
s-1
blocks.

•

OSB-word:
A symmetric-bracket word
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{k})\cdot[y,z]
is
ordered
if
z\prec y
and
z\preceq x_{i}
for all
i
, where
\prec
is the given total order on
\mathcal{C}
. We abbreviate
ordered symmetric-bracket word
to OSB-word.

•

OSBB-word:
An element
\omega=\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{s}
is called an
ordered symmetric-bracket-block word
, or an OSBB-word, if
\omega
is an SBB-word, and in addition
\omega_{i}
is ordered for
1\leq i<s
.

We make a clarifying note on the use of the word
word
. A word is an element of the natural basis of
T(W)
. The list above contains five special types of elements of homogeneous degree in
T(W)
which are strictly speaking not words, but since they are closely associated concepts, we have chosen to name them this way.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a basis of some vector space
W
, and let
\prec
be a total order on
\mathcal{C}
. Then the OSBB-words over
\mathcal{C}
form a basis of the free tensor algebra
T(W)
.

Before we are ready to prove this theorem, we need to prove the weaker claim that any word in
T(W)
can be written as a linear combination of SBB-words.

Proposition 5.2
.

Any word can be rewritten as a sum of SBB-words.

Proof.

This is immediately true for words of length 1. Assume true for words of length less than
k
and let
\omega\in T(W)
be any word of length
k
. Then
\omega-\mathfrak{s}(\omega)\in\tilde{\mathcal{J}}
by Proposition
3.1
. We get

\omega=\mathfrak{s}(\omega)+(\omega-\mathfrak{s}(\omega))=\mathfrak{s}(\omega)+\nu,

where
\nu
is a linear combination of elements on the form
\nu_{1}\cdot[x,y]\cdot\nu_{2}
with both
|\nu_{1}|<k
and
|\nu_{2}|<k
, hence we can rewrite them as a sum of SBB-words by assumption.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Step
1
.
Prove that any word can be written as a sum of OSBB-words. By Proposition
5.2
, it is sufficient to show that this is possible for any SBB-word. Assume this is true for SBB-words of length less than
k
and let
\omega
be a SBB-word of length
k
. Then
\omega=\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{s}
. Assume that
\omega_{1}=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\cdot u)\cdot[y,z]
with
u\prec z\prec y
. The Jacobi identity gives

u\cdot[y,z]=[y,z]\cdot u-y\cdot[z,u]+[z,u]\cdot y+z\cdot[y,u]-[y,u]\cdot z=:J(u\prec z\prec y).

Then we can write

\begin{split}\omega_{1}&=\frac{1}{n+1}\Biggl(\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot u\cdot[y,z]+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots\hat{x}_{i}\cdots x_{n}\cdot u)\cdot x_{i}\cdot[y,z]\Biggr)\\
&=\frac{1}{n+1}\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot J(u\prec z\prec y)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl(\frac{1}{n+1}\omega_{1}+\nu_{i}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\cdot u)\cdot[y,z]\biggr),\end{split}

where
\nu_{i}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\cdot u)\in\tilde{\mathcal{J}}
is the terms you get from rewriting
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots\hat{x}_{i}\cdots x_{n}\cdot u)\cdot x_{i}-\mathfrak{s}{(x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\cdot u)}
according to Proposition
3.1
. Solving for
\omega_{1}
, we get

\displaystyle\omega_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot J(u\prec z\prec y)+\sum_{i}\nu_{i}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\cdot u)\cdot[y,z]

(5.1)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot([y,z]\cdot u-y\cdot[z,u]+[z,u]\cdot y+z\cdot[y,u]-[y,u]\cdot z)+\sum_{i}\nu_{i}\cdot[y,z].

We need to show that each of these parts resolve into OSBB-words when concatenated with
\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}
.

•

The terms
[z,u]\cdot y
and
[y,u]\cdot z
in
J(u\prec z\prec y)
are similar and give words on the form
\eta\cdot p\cdot\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}
, where
p
is either
y
or
z
. Notice that
\eta
is an OSB-word and
|p\cdot\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}|<k
and can therefore be written as a OSBB-word by assumption.

•

The term
[y,z]\cdot u
from
J(u\prec z\prec y)
gives us a SB-word
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[y,z]
connected to a word
u\cdot\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}
. The latter part has length smaller than
k
and can be written as a OSBB-word by assumption. If
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[y,z]
is not ordered, we find the smallest element
x_{i}
and rewrite as above, but for
x_{i}
instead of
u
. Since repeating this process reduces the length of the word by one, hence the first block will resolve into an OSB-word in the end, while the rest is a word of length shorter than
k
and can therefore be rewritten as an OSBB-word.

•

Consider
y\cdot[z,u]
. By Proposition
3.1
, we get
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot y\cdot[z,u]=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\cdot y)\cdot[z,u]+\eta\cdot[z,u]
. The first term is OSB. We can write
\eta
as a sum of SBB-words by Proposition
5.2
,
\eta=\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{r}
. Since
|\eta_{2}\cdots\eta_{r}\cdot[z,u]\cdot\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}|<k
, we can rewrite this as a sum of OSBB-words by assumption. If
\eta_{1}
is not ordered, we detect the smallest element and redo this process. Since this reduces the length of the first block, the process must terminate.
The case
z\cdot[y,u]
is the same.

•

Lastly, consider
\nu_{i}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\cdot u)
. We rewrite this as a sum of SBB-words
\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{r}
. Again
\eta_{2}\cdots\eta_{r}\cdot[y,z]\cdot\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}
has length shorter than
k
and can be rewritten as an OSBB-word by assumption. If
\eta_{1}
is not OSB, we detect the smallest element and redo this process. Since the length of
\eta_{1}
is reduced by each iteration, it must terminate.

Step
2
.
Prove that the rewriting is unique. Let
\alpha_{k}(n)
denote the number of OSBB-words of length
k
made from
n
letters where the letters can be used any number of times. For convenience, let
\alpha_{0}(n)=1
for all
n
. We need to show that
\alpha_{k}(n)=n^{k}
. First, note the following recursive formula for
\alpha_{k}(n)
:

\alpha_{k}(n)=\binom{k+(n-1)}{(n-1)}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(n-j)\binom{(i-2)+(n-j)}{(n-j)}\Biggr)\alpha_{k-i}(n).

(5.2)

Each part is explained as follows:

•

\binom{k+(n-1)}{(n-1)}
is the number of symmetric words we can make of length
k
from
n
letters.

•

The
i
denotes the length of the first block in an OSBB-word that is not symmetric, hence the shortest possible length of the first block is
2
in the case it is just a bracket.

•

The
j
denotes the number of the last letter in the bracket of the first block with the enumeration being given by the order on the letters, counting from the smallest letter as number
1
, and the largest letter is then number
n
. Notice that
j
only counts until
n-1
, since there must at least be one larger letter available to put in the bracket.

•

(n-j)\binom{(i-2)+(n-j)}{(n-j)}
is the number of OSB-words of length
i
with the last letter of the bracket being number
j
counting from smallest to largest. The factor
(n-j)
corresponds to choosing a larger letter to be the first letter in the bracket, and
\binom{(i-2)+(n-j)}{(n-j)}
corresponds to choosing letters for the symmetric part, all of which is larger or equal to the last letter of the bracket.

•

If we ignore the
n
, we can read the formula as: The number of OSBB-words of length
k
is equal to the number of symmetric words of length
k
plus the number OSB-words of length
i
times the number of OSSB-words of length
k-i
for each
2\leq i\leq k
, that is, for each possible length of the first block.

We want to prove that
\alpha_{k}(n)=n^{k}
by induction on
k
. First, note that clearly
\alpha_{1}(n)=n
. Assume that
\alpha_{r}(n)=n^{r}
for all
r<k
, and note that by (
5.2
), we have

\alpha_{k-1}(n)=\binom{(k-1)+(n-1)}{(n-1)}+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\Biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(n-j)\binom{(i-2)+(n-j)}{(n-j)}\Biggr)\alpha_{k-1-i}(n).

(5.3)

By assumption,
\alpha_{k-i}(n)=n^{k-i}
as long as
i
is positive, hence
n\alpha_{k-1-i}(n)=\alpha_{k-i}(n)
. We note that the coefficient of
\alpha_{k-1-i}(n)
in (
5.3
) is equal to the coefficient of
\alpha_{k-i}(n)
in (
5.2
). These observations let us rewrite
\alpha_{k}(n)
as

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}(n)=n\alpha_{k-1}(n)+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(n-j)\binom{(k-2)+(n-j)}{(n-j)}+\binom{k+(n-1)}{(n-1)}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\alpha_k(n) =}{}-n\binom{(k-1)+(n-1)}{(n-1)}.

Here
\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(n-j)\binom{(k-2)+(n-j)}{(n-j)}
is the term in (
5.2
) corresponding to the term in the summation when
i=k
which can not be captured in
\alpha_{k-1}(n)
, and the last two terms correspond to the first term in (
5.2
) and the first term in (
5.3
). By assumption,
n\alpha_{k-1}(n)=n^{k}
, and the rest of the terms cancel by Lemma
A.1
, hence we have
\alpha_{k}(n)=n^{k}
, and the theorem follows by induction.
∎

5.1
An order on the OSBB-words

To construct OSBB-words, we depend on a total order on the elements we construct the words with. We want to use OSBB-words to construct a basis for the free algebra, but then we must construct a total order on the basis simultaneously. We start by constructing a general order on the OSBB-words, depending on the order of the elements the words are made from.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
W
be a vector space with a basis
\mathcal{C}
and a total order
\prec=\prec_{\mathcal{C}}
on
\mathcal{C}
. Define a total order
\prec_{\Delta}=\prec_{\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}}
on the set of OSBB-words in
T(W)
by

(i)

Compare symmetric words:
Let
\omega=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})
and
\eta=\mathfrak{s}(y_{1}\cdots y_{m})
be two symmetric words and let the indexing be such that
x_{1}\succeq\dots\succeq x_{n}
and
y_{1}\succeq\dots\succeq y_{m}
. We set
\omega\succ_{\Delta}\eta
if

•

n>m
, or

•

|\omega|=|\eta|
,
x_{i}=y_{i}
for
i<j
and
x_{j}\succ y_{j}
.

(ii)

Compare OSB-words:
Let
\omega=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[u,v]
and
\eta=\mathfrak{s}(y_{1}\cdots y_{m})\cdot[w,z]
be two ordered symmetric-bracket words and let the indexing be such that
x_{1}\succeq\dots\succeq x_{n}
and
y_{1}\succeq\dots\succeq y_{m}
. We set
\omega\succ_{\Delta}\eta
if

•

n>m
, or

•

n=m
and
v\succ z
, or

•

n=m
,
v=z
and
u\succ w
, or

•

n=m
,
v=z
,
u=w
, and
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\succeq_{\Delta}\mathfrak{s}(y_{1}\cdots y_{m})
.

(iii)

Compare symmetric word and OSB-word:
Let
\omega=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[u,v]
be an OSB-word and
\eta=\mathfrak{s}(y_{1}\cdots y_{m})
a symmetric word, again assuming the
x_{i}
’s and
y_{i}
’s are listed in decreasing order. Then
\omega\succ_{\Delta}\eta
if and only if

•

|\omega|>|\eta|
.

(iv)

Compare OSBB-words:
let
\omega=\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{k}
and
\eta=\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{r}
be two OSBB-words. Let
\omega\succ_{\Delta}\eta
if

•

|\omega|>|\eta|
, or

•

|\omega|=|\eta|
,
\omega_{i}=\eta_{i}
for
i<j
and
\omega_{j}\succ_{\Delta}\eta_{j}
.

The following lemma is a generalization of a normal quality of the lexicographical order on words: if one letter in a word is replaced by a smaller letter (lets say we replace an “A” with a “B”) the resulting word would be smaller. When considering OSBB-words, the case becomes a bit more technical, since replacing letters with smaller letters means the resulting word need not be an OSBB-word.

An OSBB-word
\omega
will consist of
|\omega|
elements from the underlying algebra put together by
\mathfrak{s}
,
[\,\_\,,\,\_\,]
and
\cdot
. If
\omega=\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{1}^{1}\cdots x_{n_{1}}^{1}\bigr)\cdot\bigl[y^{1},z^{1}\bigr]\cdots\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x^{k}_{1}\cdots x^{k}_{n_{k}}\bigr)
we introduce the notation
\bar{\omega}\bigl(u_{1},\dots,u_{|\omega|}\bigr)=\mathfrak{s}\bigl(u_{1}\cdots u_{n_{1}}\bigr)\cdot\bigl[u_{n_{1}+1},u_{n_{1}+2}\bigr]\cdots\mathfrak{s}\bigl(u_{|\omega|-n_{k}}\cdots u_{|\omega|}\bigr)
, by which we mean: maintain the same structure of the word with respect to
\mathfrak{s}
,
[\,\_\,,\,\_\,]
and
\cdot
, but exchange the
i
’th input with
u_{i}
. Notice that
\bar{\omega}\bigl(u_{1},\dots,u_{|\omega|}\bigr)
need not be an OSBB-word since the condition that the second argument of each bracket must be minimal in its block might not be satisfied.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\omega=\bar{\omega}\bigl(u_{1},\dots,u_{|\omega|}\bigr)
be an OSBB-word, and let
v_{i}\preceq u_{i}
for all
i
. Rewrite
\bar{\omega}\bigl(v_{1},\dots,v_{|\omega|}\bigr)
as a linear combination of OSBB-words. Then each term
\eta
in this linear expansion satisfies
\eta\preceq_{\Delta}\omega
.

Proof.

We do this by induction on the length
|\omega|
. The statement is clearly true for
|\omega|=1
, and assume it is true whenever
|\omega|<k
. Consider the case
|\omega|=k
. We introduce the notation
\{u_{i}\leftrightarrow v_{i}\}
to mean that
u_{i}
is replaced by
v_{i}
. Notice that rewriting
\bar{\omega}\bigl(u_{1},\dots,\{u_{i}\leftrightarrow v_{i}\},\dots,u_{|\omega|}\bigr)
as a sum of OSBB-words
\eta_{j}
, then changing
\{u_{l}\leftrightarrow v_{l}\}
in each of the OSBB-words
\eta_{j}
and then rewriting all those terms as OSBB-words will give the same result as rewriting
\bar{\omega}\bigl(u_{1},\dots,\{u_{i}\leftrightarrow v_{i}\},\dots,\{u_{l}\leftrightarrow v_{l}\},\dots,u_{|\omega|}\bigr)
into a linear combination of OSBB-words directly. This is because we have equality at each step, and the rewriting into OSBB-words is unique. Hence it is sufficient to show that the lemma holds when
u_{i}=v_{i}
for all
i
except one. Furthermore, if
\omega=\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{s}
and we exchange an element from another block than
\omega_{1}
, by assumption the lemma holds for the OSBB-word
\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}
, and we get
\eta=\omega_{1}\cdot\eta_{0}
where
\eta_{0}
is the result after solving the reduced problem. Then
\eta_{0}\preceq_{\Delta}\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}
implies
\eta\preceq_{\Delta}\omega
.

Let
\omega_{1}=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[y,z]
.

•

Let
\tilde{z}\preceq z
, then
\tilde{\omega}_{1}\cdot\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[y,\tilde{z}]
is an ordered-symmetric-bracket block and
\tilde{\omega}_{1}\cdot\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}\preceq_{\Delta}\omega
.

•

Let
\tilde{y}\preceq y
, then
\tilde{\omega}_{1}\cdot\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[\tilde{y},z]
is either an OSB-word, equal to zero (if
\tilde{y}=z
), or
\tilde{\omega}_{1}=-\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[z,\tilde{y}]
is an OSB-word. In all cases
\tilde{\omega}_{1}\cdot\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}\preceq_{\Delta}\omega
.

•

If
\tilde{x}_{i}\preceq x_{i}
, we must rewrite
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots\{x_{i}\leftrightarrow\tilde{x}_{i}\}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[y,z]\cdot\omega_{2}\cdots\omega_{s}
as a sum of OSBB-words. If
\tilde{x}_{i}\succeq z
, we are done. If not, we must consider equation (
5.1
) from the proof of Theorem
5.1
. By inspection, we can see that the first block after rewriting will either have
\tilde{x}_{i}
as the last term of the bracket or have shorter length than
\omega_{1}
, and we get
\eta\preceq_{\Delta}\omega
.

This concludes the proof.
∎

6
Using OSBB-words to build bases for the free algebra

The basis
\mathcal{B}
of
\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})
presented in Theorem
3.6
is not naturally compatible with the tree basis
\overrightarrow{\operatorname{Tree}}(\mathcal{C})
of the free algebra. In fact, it is not a priori clear that the elements of
\mathcal{B}
are linearly independent in
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
as the elements in
\mathcal{B}
could be very complicated to write in the basis
\overrightarrow{\operatorname{Tree}}(\mathcal{C})
. By using Theorem
5.1
, we will be able to construct a basis for
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
in which
\mathcal{B}
is a subset, hence we conclude at the end of this section that the suggested basis for
\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})
is at least a linearly independent set.

6.1
An ordered basis of
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})}

The tree basis for the free algebra
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
is the set of planar rooted trees colored by the set
\mathcal{C}
. Any tree
\tau
in this basis will have a specific number of vertices, and we let
|\tau|_{V}
denote this number: The number of vertices
|\,\_\,|_{V}
of a tree in
\overrightarrow{\operatorname{Tree}}(\mathcal{C})
is given by

•

|c|_{V}=1
for any
c\in\mathcal{C}
, and

•

|(t_{1}\cdots t_{n})\rhd c|_{V}=1+|t_{1}|_{V}+\dots+|t_{n}|_{V}
for
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in\overrightarrow{\operatorname{Tree}}(\mathcal{C})
.

Then the free algebra
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
is a naturally graded vector space

\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\infty}V_{i},

where
V_{k}
is the vector space spanned by all the trees with
k
vertices. This definition can immediately be generalized to the standard basis elements of the D-algebra by
|t_{1}\cdots t_{n}|_{V}=|t_{1}|_{V}+\dots+|t_{n}|_{V}
. Furthermore, it is clear that rewriting an element
\omega
from the standard basis preserves both the length of the word
|\omega|
and the number of vertices
|\omega|_{V}
, hence
|\,\_\,|_{V}
is well defined for OSBB-words of elements on which
|\,\_\,|_{V}
is well defined. Lastly, if
\omega
is an OSBB-word and
c\in\mathcal{C}
, we may define
|\omega\rhd c|_{V}=1+|\omega|_{V}
, something that corresponds to the notion that
\omega\rhd c\in V_{k}
where
k=1+|\omega|_{V}
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a totally ordered set. Consider the following set
\mathcal{S}\subset\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
:

•

if
c\in\mathcal{C}
then
c\in\mathcal{S}
,

•

if
\omega
is an OSBB-word of elements from
\mathcal{S}
, then
\omega\rhd c\in\mathcal{S}
,

where we use the order
\prec_{\mathcal{S}}
defined by
\omega\rhd c_{1}\prec_{\mathcal{S}}\eta\rhd c_{2}
if

(i)

|\omega|_{V}<|\eta|_{V}
, or

(ii)

|\omega|_{V}=|\eta|_{V}
and
\omega\prec_{\Delta}\eta
, or

(iii)

\omega=\eta
and
c_{1}<c_{2}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

The set
\mathcal{S}
is a basis for
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{\mathcal{C}})
.

Proof.

By induction on
|\,\_\,|_{V}
. Since
c\in\mathcal{S}
for any
c\in\mathcal{C}
, and this is a basis for
V_{1}
, we see that the elements in
\mathcal{S}
with
|x|_{V}=1
is indeed a basis for
V_{1}
. Assume that the elements
y\in\mathcal{S}
with
|y|_{V}=j
is an ordered basis for
V_{j}
for all
j<k
. Thus by assumption, any tree in the tree basis of
V_{j}
can be written uniquely as a linear combination of elements in
\mathcal{S}
.

Step
1
.
Show that any element in
V_{k}
can be written uniquely as a linear combination of elements in
\mathcal{S}
. Let
x\in V_{k}
be a planar rooted tree in the tree basis for
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
. Then
x=(t_{1}\cdots t_{n})\rhd c
. By assumption, each
t_{i}
can be uniquely rewritten as a linear combination of
\mathcal{S}
giving
x=\sum_{i}a_{i}(\omega_{i}\rhd c)
, where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\omega_{i}
are words of elements form
\mathcal{S}
satisfying
|\omega_{i}|=n
and
|\omega_{i}|_{V}=k-1
. By Theorem
5.1
, each of these words
\omega_{i}
can be uniquely rewritten as a linear combination of OSBB-words, since
\omega_{i}
is a word of elements
y^{i}_{j}
from
\mathcal{S}
with
\bigl|y^{i}_{j}\bigr|_{V}<k
, and these are ordered by assumption. this means that any element in
V_{k}
can be uniquely written as a linear combination of elements from
\mathcal{S}
.

Step
2
.
Show that the order
\prec_{\mathcal{S}}
is well defined on elements in
V_{k}
. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{S}
be two elements with
|x|_{V}=|y|_{V}=k
. Then
x=\omega\rhd c_{1}
and
y=\eta\rhd c_{2}
for some OSBB-words
\omega
and
\eta
of elements in
\mathcal{S}
with less than
k
vertices. We may compare
x
and
y
by comparing
\omega
and
\eta
using
\prec_{\Delta}
and by comparing
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
using the given total order on
\mathcal{C}
. Notice that the order on
\mathcal{S}
prioritize the number of vertices, hence
x
is clearly comparable to any
z\in V_{j}
for
j<k
.

By induction on the number of vertices, the elements
x
of
\mathcal{S}
with
|x|_{V}=i
is an ordered basis of
V_{i}
for any
i
, hence
\mathcal{S}
is a totally ordered basis for
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

6.2
An ordered basis for
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})}
that contains
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}

We will now define another basis for the free algebra that is the one that most closely capture the important features for understanding the Lie admissible triple algebra. Although the basis
\mathcal{S}
does give an understanding of the intrinsic relations that follows from the definition of the triple-bracket, it is not a convenient basis when working with the LAT-relations.

Definition 6.2
.

Define a map
\psi
by

\displaystyle\psi\colon\ \mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C}),

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\longmapsto c,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathfrak{s}(\psi(x_{1})\cdots\psi(x_{n}))\rhd c,

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[y,z]\cdot\omega)\rhd c

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathfrak{s}(\psi(x_{1})\cdots\psi(x_{n}))\rhd[\psi(y),\psi(z),\psi(\omega\rhd c)]

and extend to
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
by linearity.

Proposition 6.3
.

The map
\psi
is a linear automorphism of
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

To prove this, we start by showing that
\psi(x)=x+q
where
q
is a linear combination of elements in
\mathcal{S}
that is smaller than
x
. This is clearly true for
c\in V_{1}
. Assume this is true for
y\in V_{j}
with
j<k
, and let
x\in V_{k}
. There are two cases:

•

If
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c
, we have
\psi(x)=\mathfrak{s}(\psi(x_{1})\cdots\psi(x_{n}))\rhd c
. By assumption,
\psi(x_{i})=x_{i}+q_{i}
, and since all the terms in
q_{i}
are smaller than
x_{i}
by assumption, we immediately have
\psi(x)=x+q
, where all the terms in
q
are smaller than
x
.

•

If
x=(\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[y,z]\cdot\omega)\rhd c
, we have
\psi(x)=\mathfrak{s}(\psi(x_{1})\cdots\psi(x_{n}))\rhd[\psi(y),\psi(z),\psi(\omega\rhd c)]
. By assumption,
\psi(x_{i})=x_{i}+q_{i}
,
\psi(y)=y+p
,
\psi(z)=z+r
and
\psi(\omega\rhd c)=\omega\rhd c+s
. It is clear that the terms in
s
has the form
\eta\rhd c
. We need to show that if
\tilde{x}_{i}\preceq x_{i}
,
\tilde{y}\preceq y
,
\tilde{z}\preceq z
and
\eta\preceq_{\Delta}\omega
, then

\mathfrak{s}(\tilde{x}_{1}\cdots\tilde{x}_{n})\cdot[\tilde{y},\tilde{z}]\cdot\eta\preceq_{\Delta}\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\cdot[y,z]\cdot\omega.

This follows from Lemma
5.4
.

This means that the map
\psi
acts as an upper triangular matrix with
1
’s on the diagonal on each graded component
V_{i}
with respect to the ordered basis
\mathcal{S}
. Clearly such a matrix is invertible, hence
\psi
is an automorphism.
∎

Let
\mathcal{D}
be defined as the image of
\mathcal{S}
by the map
\psi
. Then
\mathcal{D}
is a basis of
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
. We may define
\mathcal{D}
iteratively by

(i)

if
c\in\mathcal{C}
, then
c\in\mathcal{D}
,

(ii)

if
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\dots x_{n})\rhd c
, where
x_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
, then
x\in\mathcal{D}
,

(iii)

if
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\dots x_{n})\rhd[y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}]
, where
x_{i},y_{j}\in\mathcal{D}
with
y_{1}>y_{2}
and
y_{2}\leq x_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
and
j=1,2,3
, then
x\in\mathcal{D}
.

It is sufficient to compare this definition with the image of
\psi
to see that the two definitions of
\mathcal{D}
are the same. The set
\mathcal{D}
is a totally ordered set by
\psi(x)\prec\psi(y)
if and only if
x\prec_{\mathcal{S}}y
.

Corollary 6.4
.

The set
\mathcal{B}
from Theorem
3.6
is linearly independent in
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Observe that
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{D}
.
∎

7
Proof of Theorem
3.6

To prove Theorem
3.6
, we will construct a linear map
\phi\colon\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
such that
\ker(\phi)=\mathcal{I}
and
\operatorname{Im}(\phi)=\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
. The map will be defined explicitly (or iteratively) on the basis
\mathcal{D}
.

Let
\pi_{D}
be a linear projection defined by

\displaystyle\pi_{D}\colon\ \operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C}),

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\longmapsto c

\displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\pi_{D}(x_{1}),\pi_{D}(x_{2}),\pi_{D}(x_{3})],

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{D}(x_{1})\cdots\pi_{D}(x_{n}))\rhd c,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\pi_{D}\biggl(\sum_{I,J,K}[\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd y_{1},\mathfrak{s}(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}})\rhd y_{2},

\displaystyle\hphantom{\longmapsto\pi_D \biggl( \sum_{I,J,K}}\mathfrak{s}(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}})\rhd y_{3}]\biggr),

where
I
,
J
,
K
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
I\cup J\cup K=\{1,\dots,n\}
, and for any
s<t
we have
i_{s}<i_{t}
,
j_{s}<j_{t}
and
k_{s}<k_{t}
. Observe that
\operatorname{Im}(\pi_{D})
contains no elements on the form
\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}]
. Define a set

\bar{\mathcal{D}}=\{x\in\mathcal{D}\mid x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c,\,x_{i}\in\mathcal{D}\}\subset\mathcal{D}

and note that
(\operatorname{Im}(\pi_{D}),[\,\_\,,\,\_\,,\,\_\,])\subset\operatorname{Alg}_{3}(\bar{\mathcal{D}})
, hence we may define a Hall map
\mathfrak{H}
as in Definition
4.3
and restrict it to the image of
\pi_{D}
.

Let
\pi_{H}
be the projections defined by

\displaystyle\pi_{H}\colon\ \operatorname{Im}(\pi_{D})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C}),

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\longmapsto c,

\displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathfrak{H}([\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})]),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{H}(x_{1})\cdots\pi_{H}(x_{n}))\rhd c.

Note 7.1
.

The notation
\pi_{D}
and
\pi_{H}
is chosen for the following reasons: Firstly, they are both linear projections, which we indicate by the letter
\pi
. The projection
\pi_{D}
is constructed to account for the relation (
2.2
), which is a derivation property by the triangle product on the triple-bracket, hence the letter
D
. In particular, this choice is not connected to the set
\mathcal{D}
. The letter
H
in
\pi_{H}
is there to indicate its relation with Hall bases.

Proposition 7.2
.

For any
x\in\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
, we have
x-\pi_{D}(x)\in\mathcal{I}
.

By Lemma
B.1
, we know that

\displaystyle(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\in\mathcal{I},

where
I
,
J
,
K
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
I\cup J\cup K=\{1,\dots,n\}
, and for any
s<t
we have
i_{s}<i_{t}
,
j_{s}<j_{t}
and
k_{s}<k_{t}
. In this proof we will always let
I
,
J
and
K
denote a triplet of sets with these properties.

Proof.

By induction on
|x|_{V}
: If
|x|_{V}=1
, we have
x\in\mathcal{C}
and
x-\pi_{D}(x)=x-x=0\in\mathcal{I}
. Assume
x-\pi_{D}(x)\in\mathcal{I}
when
|x|_{V}<k
, then consider the case
|x|_{V}=k
. By linearity of
\pi_{D}
, we may assume
x\in\mathcal{D}
. If
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c
, we have

x-\pi_{D}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{D}(x_{1})\cdots(x_{i}-\pi_{D}(x_{i}))\cdots x_{n})\rhd c.

Since
|x_{i}|_{V}<k
, we have
x_{i}-\pi_{D}(x_{i})\in\mathcal{I}
, hence also
x-\pi_{D}(x)\in\mathcal{I}
. If
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]
, we have

\displaystyle x-\pi_{D}(x)=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{x - \pi_D(x) =}{}-\pi_{D}\biggl(\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\biggr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{x - \pi_D(x) }{}=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{x - \pi_D(x) =}{}-\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]

\displaystyle\hphantom{x - \pi_D(x) =}{}+\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]

\displaystyle\hphantom{x - \pi_D(x) =}{}-\pi_{D}\biggl(\sum_{I,J,K}[\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}]\biggr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{x - \pi_D(x)}{}=\alpha+\sum_{I,J,K}[\beta_{I}-\pi_{D}(\beta_{I}),\beta_{J},\beta_{K}]+[\pi_{D}(\beta_{I}),\beta_{J}-\pi_{D}(\beta_{J}),\beta_{K}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{x - \pi_D(x) =\alpha+ \sum_{I,J,K}}{}+[\pi_{D}(\beta_{I}),\pi_{D}(\beta_{J}),\beta_{K}-\pi_{D}(\beta_{K})],

where

\displaystyle\alpha=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{\alpha=}{}-\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\in\mathcal{I}

by Lemma
B.1
and

\displaystyle\beta_{I}=\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\qquad\beta_{J}=\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\qquad\beta_{K}=\mathfrak{s}\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}.

Since
|\beta_{t}|_{V}<p
, we have
\beta_{t}-\pi_{D}(\beta_{t})\in\mathcal{I}
for
t=I,J,K
, hence
x-\pi_{D}(x)\in\mathcal{I}
.
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

If
x\in\operatorname{Im}(\pi_{D})
, then
x-\pi_{H}(x)\in\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

If
|x|_{V}=1
, we have
x-\pi_{H}(x)=0\in\mathcal{I}
by definition, since
x\in\mathcal{C}
. Assume the proposition holds for all
x
with
|x|_{V}<k
, then consider the case
|x|_{V}=k
. If
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c
, we have

x-\pi_{H}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{s}(\pi_{H}(x_{1})\cdots(x_{i}-\pi_{H}(x_{i}))\cdots x_{n})\rhd c,

where
|x_{i}|_{V}<k
, hence
x_{i}-\pi_{H}(x_{i})\in\mathcal{I}
by assumption, and we get
x-\pi_{H}(x)\in\mathcal{I}
. If
x=[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]
, we have

\displaystyle x-\pi_{H}(x)=[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]-\mathfrak{H}([\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})])

\displaystyle\hphantom{x- \pi_H(x)}{}=[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]-[\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})]+[\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})]

\displaystyle\hphantom{x- \pi_H(x)=}{}-\mathfrak{H}([\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})])

\displaystyle\hphantom{x- \pi_H(x)}{}=[x_{1}-\pi_{H}(x_{1}),x_{2},x_{3}]+[\pi_{H}(x_{1}),x_{2}-\pi_{H}(x_{2}),x_{3}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{x- \pi_H(x)=}{}+[\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),x_{3}-\pi_{H}(x_{3})]+([\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})]

\displaystyle\hphantom{x- \pi_H(x)=}{}-\mathfrak{H}([\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})])).

Since
x_{i}-\pi_{H}(x_{i})\in\mathcal{I}
by assumption and

[\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})]-\mathfrak{H}([\pi_{H}(x_{1}),\pi_{H}(x_{2}),\pi_{H}(x_{3})])\in\mathcal{I}

by the definition of
\mathfrak{H}
, we conclude that
x-\pi_{H}(x)\in\mathcal{I}
. The proposition follows by induction.
∎

Let
\phi:=\pi_{H}\circ\pi_{D}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

The ideal
\mathcal{I}
is the kernel of
\phi
.

Proof.

Step
1
.
It is clear by the definition of
\pi_{D}
that
\phi(x)
can never contain a term or a factor of a term on the form

(y_{1}\cdots y_{n})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

and hence
\phi(x)
can never contain a factor satisfying

u\rhd[v,w,z]-[u\rhd v,w,z]-[v,u\rhd w,z]-[v,w,u\rhd z].

It is also clear by the definition of
\pi_{H}
that
\phi(x)
can not contain a non-Hall triple-bracket as a term or a factor of a term, hence
\phi(x)
can never contain a factor of the form

[u,v,w]+[v,w,u]+[w,u,v].

We conclude that either
\phi(x)=0
or
\phi(x)\notin\mathcal{I}
.

Step
2
.
Let
x\in\mathcal{I}
and assume
\phi(x)\neq 0
. By Propositions
7.2
and
7.3
, we know that
x-\phi(x)\in\mathcal{I}
, but by step 1 we have
\phi(x)\notin\mathcal{I}
. This is a contradiction and we conclude that
\phi(x)=0
, which proves that
\mathcal{I}\subset\ker(\phi)
.

Step
3
.
Now, let
x
be any element in
\ker(\phi)
. By Propositions
7.2
and
7.3
, we know that
x-\phi(x)\in\mathcal{I}
, but
\phi(x)=0
, hence
x\in\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}\subset\ker(\phi)
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

The image of
\phi
is equal to the span of
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

By induction on
|\,\_\,|_{V}
. If
x\in\mathcal{B}
with
|x|_{V}=1
, then
\phi(x)=x
since
\phi(c)=c
for all elements
c\in\mathcal{C}
.

(i)

If
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c
, we have by induction

\phi(x)=\mathfrak{s}(\phi(x_{1})\cdots\phi(x_{n}))\rhd c=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c=x.

(ii)

If
x=[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]
, we have by induction

\phi(x)=[\phi(x_{1}),\phi(x_{2}),\phi(x_{3})]=[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]=x.

Hence we get
\phi(x)=x
for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
which proves that
\mathcal{B}\subset\operatorname{Im}(\phi)
.
Notice that for
|x|_{V}=1
we have
\phi(x)\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
. Assume
\phi(y)\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
for all
y\in\mathcal{D}
with
|y|_{V}<k
and let
x\in\mathcal{D}
with
|x|_{V}=k
. If
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd c
, we get
\phi(x)=\mathfrak{s}(\phi(x_{1})\cdots\phi(x_{n}))\rhd c
and since
|x_{i}|_{V}<|x|_{V}
, we have
\phi(x_{i})\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
by assumption, and then
\phi(x)
must be in the span of
\mathcal{B}
.

If
x=\mathfrak{s}(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}]
, we have

\phi(x)=\sum_{i\in I}\mathfrak{H}([\phi(\alpha_{i}),\phi(\beta_{i}),\phi(\gamma_{i})]),

where
\alpha_{i}
,
\beta_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}
are as described in the definition of
\pi_{D}
and
i\in I
is any indexing of these elements. Since
|\alpha_{i}|_{V}<|x|_{V}
,
|\beta_{i}|_{V}<|x|_{V}
and
|\gamma_{i}|_{V}<|x|_{V}
for all
i\in I
, we know that
\phi(\alpha_{i})\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
,
\phi(\beta_{i})\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
and
\phi(\gamma_{i})\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
for all
i\in I
, and furthermore, by the definition of
\mathfrak{H}
we know that
\mathfrak{H}([\phi(\alpha_{i}),\phi(\beta_{i}),\phi(\gamma_{i})])
will be a Hall Lie triple element of elements from
\mathcal{B}
, hence it is itself in
\mathcal{B}
.
This means that
\phi(x)\in\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}
, and since
\mathcal{D}
is a basis of
\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
, we know that
\operatorname{Im}(\phi)\subset\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

Since
\phi
is a linear map, we have

\operatorname{Im}(\phi)\cong\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})/\ker(\phi).

By Lemma
7.5
,
\operatorname{Im}(\phi)=\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}
, and by Lemma
7.4
,
\ker(\phi)=\mathcal{I}
. By definition,
\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})\cong\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})/\mathcal{I}
and we have

\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})\cong\operatorname{span}\{\mathcal{B}\}.

Furthermore, by Corollary
6.4
we know that the set
\mathcal{B}
is linearly independent, hence
\mathcal{B}
is a basis for the free Lie admissible triple algebra.
∎

8
Embedding of LAT into post-Lie

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a Lie algebra with an involutive automorphism
\sigma\colon\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
, that is an isomorphism satisfying
\sigma^{2}(x)=x
for all
x\in\mathcal{L}
. Then
\mathcal{L}
decomposes into
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{-}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{+}
where
\mathcal{L}_{-}:=\{x\in\mathcal{L}\mid\sigma(x)=-x\}
and
\mathcal{L}_{+}:=\{x\in\mathcal{L}\mid\sigma(x)=x\}
. We observe that

[\mathcal{L}_{+},\mathcal{L}_{+}]\subset\mathcal{L}_{+},\qquad[\mathcal{L}_{-},\mathcal{L}_{+}]\subset\mathcal{L}_{-},\qquad[\mathcal{L}_{-},\mathcal{L}_{-}]\subset\mathcal{L}_{+}.

In other words,
\mathcal{L}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Lie algebra, and we may define a triple-bracket

[\,\_\,,\,\_\,,\,\_\,]\colon\ \mathcal{L}_{-}\times\mathcal{L}_{-}\times\mathcal{L}_{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{-}

by
[x,y,z]:=[[x,y],z]
. This makes
\mathcal{L}_{-}
into a Lie triple system, defined in Lemma
2.4
. The following proposition is originally due to Jacobson
[
14
]
, and improved by Yamaguti
[
28
]
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
T
be a Lie triple system. For
x,y\in T
let
D_{x,y}
be the linear transformation
D_{x,y}(z)=[x,y,z]
. Let
\operatorname{Der}(T)
be the subspace of all linear transformations of
T
spanned by
D_{x,y}
, and let
\mathcal{L}
be the direct sum vector space
\operatorname{Der}(T)\oplus T
. Then
\mathcal{L}
becomes a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Lie algebra with the bracket

[(A,x),(B,y)]=(A\circ B-B\circ A+D_{x,y},A(y)-B(x)).

The map
\sigma
defined by
\sigma((A,x))=(A,-x)
is an involutive automorphism.

Proof.

See
[
15
, Proposition 2.3]
.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=\operatorname{Der}(T)\oplus T
be as in the proposition above, but define the bracket by

[(A,x),(B,y)]=(-A\circ B+B\circ A+D_{x,y},B(x)-A(y)).

This will also define a Lie algebra, and
\sigma\colon(A,x)\mapsto(A,-x)
is an involutive automorphism of this Lie algebra as well.

It is natural to ask how this theorem extends to Lie admissible triple algebras. The relation between LAT and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Lie algebras have been examined in
[
11
,
25
]
. This embedding can be further specialized by demanding that the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Lie algebra has a post-Lie structure, and that the products of the Lie admissible triple algebra agrees with the post-Lie product.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
(A,[\,\_\,,\,\_\,],\blacktriangleright)
be a post-Lie algebra. Define a product
\rhd
by

x\rhd y=x\blacktriangleright y+\frac{1}{2}[x,y],

then
(A,\rhd)
is Lie admissible. The skew associator is

\operatorname{ass}_{\rhd}(x,y,z)-\operatorname{ass}_{\rhd}(y,x,z)=-\frac{1}{4}[[x,y],z].

Proof.

See
[
19
, Proposition 2.7]
.
∎

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
(A,[\,\_\,,\,\_\,],\blacktriangleright)
be a post-Lie algebra. Define a product
\rhd
by

x\rhd y=x\blacktriangleright y+\frac{1}{2}[x,y],

then
(A,\rhd)
is a Lie admissible triple algebra.

Proof.

Define

[x,y,z]:=\operatorname{ass}_{\rhd}(x,y,z)-\operatorname{ass}_{\rhd}(y,x,z).

By Proposition
8.3
above, we know that

[x,y,z]+[y,z,x]+[z,x,y]=-\frac{1}{4}([[x,y],z]+[[y,z],x]+[[z,x],y])=0.

We need to show that
\rhd
acts as a derivation on
[\,\_\,,\,\_\,,\,\_\,]
,

\displaystyle w\rhd[x,y,z]=w\blacktriangleright\biggl(-\frac{1}{4}[[x,y],z]\biggr)+\frac{1}{2}[w,-\frac{1}{4}[[x,y],z]]

\displaystyle\hphantom{w \rhd[x,y,z] }{}=-\frac{1}{4}([w\blacktriangleright[x,y],z]+[[x,y],w\blacktriangleright z])+\frac{1}{2}\biggl(-\frac{1}{4}([[w,[x,y]],z]+[[x,y],[w,z]])\biggr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{w \rhd[x,y,z] }{}=-\frac{1}{4}\biggl([[w\blacktriangleright x,y],z]+[[x,w\blacktriangleright y],z]+[[x,y],w\blacktriangleright z]

\displaystyle\hphantom{w \rhd[x,y,z] =-\frac{1}{4}}{}+\frac{1}{2}([[[w,x],y],z]+[[x,[w,y]],z]+[[x,y],[w,z]])\biggr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{w \rhd[x,y,z] }{}=-\frac{1}{4}\biggl([[w\blacktriangleright x,y],z]+\frac{1}{2}[[[w,x],y],z]\biggr)-\frac{1}{4}\biggl([[x,w\blacktriangleright y],z]+\frac{1}{2}[[x,[w,y]],z]\biggr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{w \rhd[x,y,z] =}{}-\frac{1}{4}\biggl([[x,y],w\blacktriangleright z]+\frac{1}{2}[[x,y],[w,z]]\biggr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{w \rhd[x,y,z] }{}=[w\rhd x,y,z]+[x,w\rhd y,z]+[x,y,w\rhd z].

∎

Example 8.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\rhd)
be a LAT-algebra, hence it is also a Lie admissible algebra. Define
[x,y]=x\rhd y-y\rhd x
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{A}
, then
(\mathcal{A},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,])
is a Lie algebra. For any Lie algebra if we define
x\blacktriangleright y:=[y,x]
, we get a post-Lie algebra, hence
(\mathcal{A},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,],\blacktriangleright)
is a post-Lie algebra where
x\blacktriangleright y=y\rhd x-x\rhd y
.

This is not the embedding we are interested in, as this is not related to the Lie triple embedding in Proposition
8.1
. What we want is to embed the Lie triple algebra
(\mathcal{A},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,,\,\_\,])
into a Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}=\operatorname{Der}(\mathcal{A})\oplus\mathcal{A}
, and then extend the Lie admissible triple operator
\rhd
to an operator
\blacktriangleright
on
\mathcal{L}
in such a way that

(i)

(\mathcal{L},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,],\blacktriangleright)
is a post-Lie algebra,

(ii)

and the post-Lie operator reduces to the LAT operator when restricted to
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,

\blacktriangleright\big|_{\mathcal{A}}=\rhd.

If we let
(\mathcal{L},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,])
be the standard Lie algebra embedding of the Lie triple system
(\mathcal{A},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,,\,\_\,])
, where
(\mathcal{A},\rhd)
is a LAT algebra. Using the post-Lie relations, we see that the extension must satisfy

\displaystyle x\blacktriangleright y=x\rhd y,

\displaystyle D_{x,y}\blacktriangleright z=[x,y]\blacktriangleright z=[x,y,z]=D_{x,y}(z),

\displaystyle x\blacktriangleright D_{y,z}=x\blacktriangleright[y,z]=[x\blacktriangleright y,z]+[y,x\blacktriangleright z]=D_{x\blacktriangleright y,z}+D_{y,x\blacktriangleright z},

\displaystyle D_{x,y}\blacktriangleright D_{u,v}=[x,y]\blacktriangleright[u,v]=[[x,y,u],v]+[u,[x,y,v]]=D_{[x,y,u],v}+D_{u,[x,y,v]}.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\rhd)
be a Lie admissible triple algebra with triple-bracket

[x,y,z]=\operatorname{ass}_{\rhd}(x,y,z)-\operatorname{ass}_{\rhd}(y,x,z).

For each pair
x,y\in\mathcal{A}
, define

\displaystyle D_{x,y}\colon\ \mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A},\qquad z\longmapsto[x,y,z]

and let
\operatorname{Der}(\mathcal{A})=\{D_{x,y}\mid x,y\in\mathcal{A}\}
. Let
\mathcal{L}=\operatorname{Der}(\mathcal{A})\oplus\mathcal{A}
, and let
x,y\in\mathcal{A}
and
A,B\in\operatorname{Der}(\mathcal{A})
. Define a bracket by

\displaystyle[x,y]=D_{x,y},\qquad[A,x]=-A(x),\qquad[A,B]=-A\circ B+B\circ A.

Extend
\rhd
to all of
\mathcal{L}
by

\displaystyle x\blacktriangleright y=x\rhd y,\qquad A\blacktriangleright x=A(x),

\displaystyle(x\blacktriangleright A)(y)=x\blacktriangleright A(y)-A(x\blacktriangleright y),\qquad A\blacktriangleright B=A\circ B-B\circ A.

Then
(\mathcal{L},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,],\blacktriangleright)
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded post-Lie algebra.

For the sake of convenience, we will write
\operatorname{ass}_{\blacktriangleright}(x,y,z)-\operatorname{ass}_{\blacktriangleright}(y,x,z)=[x,y,z]
.

Proof.

(\mathcal{L},[\,\_\,,\,\_\,])
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Lie algebra by Proposition
8.1
and Remark
8.2
. Throughout this proof, let
x,y,z,w,t\in\mathcal{A}
and
A,B,C\in\operatorname{Der}(\mathcal{A})
. From the definition of
(x\blacktriangleright A)
, we get

\displaystyle(x\blacktriangleright D_{y,z})(w)

\displaystyle=x\blacktriangleright(D_{y,z}(w))-D_{y,z}(x\blacktriangleright w)=x\blacktriangleright[y,z,w]-[y,z,x\blacktriangleright w]

\displaystyle=[x\blacktriangleright y,z,w]+[y,x\blacktriangleright z,w]=(D_{x\blacktriangleright y,z}+D_{y,x\blacktriangleright z})(w),

which gives the equality

x\blacktriangleright D_{y,z}=D_{x\blacktriangleright y,z}+D_{y,x\blacktriangleright z}.

(8.1)

From the definition of
A\blacktriangleright B
, we get

\displaystyle(D_{x,y}\blacktriangleright D_{z,w})(t)

\displaystyle=(D_{x,y}\circ D_{z,w}-D_{z,w}\circ D_{x,y})(t)=[x,y,[z,w,t]]-[z,w,[x,y,t]]

\displaystyle=[[x,y,z],w,t]+[z,[x,y,w],t]=(D_{[x,y,z],w}+D_{z,[x,y,w]})(t),

which gives the equality

D_{x,y}\blacktriangleright D_{z,w}=D_{[x,y,z],w}+D_{z,[x,y,w]}.

(8.2)

We need to verify that the post-Lie relations are satisfied for all combinations of inputs from
\operatorname{Der}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{A}
.

(i) Using (
8.1
), we get

x\blacktriangleright[y,z]=x\blacktriangleright D_{y,z}=D_{x\blacktriangleright y,z}+D_{y,x\blacktriangleright z}=[x\blacktriangleright y,z]+[y,x\blacktriangleright z].

(ii) By the definition of
x\blacktriangleright A
, we get

x\blacktriangleright[A,y]=-x\blacktriangleright(A(y))=-(x\blacktriangleright A)(y)-A(x\blacktriangleright y)=[x\blacktriangleright A,y]+[A,x\blacktriangleright y].

(iii) Follows from skew symmetry and the relation above

x\blacktriangleright[y,A]=-x\blacktriangleright[A,y]=-[x\blacktriangleright A,y]-[A,x\blacktriangleright y]=[x\blacktriangleright y,A]+[y,x\blacktriangleright A].

(iv) Let
B=D_{y,z}
. Using (i) and (ii), we can compute

\displaystyle x\blacktriangleright[A,B]={}

\displaystyle x\blacktriangleright[A,[y,z]]=x\blacktriangleright([[A,y],z]+[y,[A,z]])

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle[x\blacktriangleright[A,y],z]+[[A,y],x\blacktriangleright z]+[x\blacktriangleright y,[A,z]]+[y,x\blacktriangleright[A,z]]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle[[x\blacktriangleright A,y],z]+[[A,x\blacktriangleright y],z]+[[A,y],x\blacktriangleright z]+[x\blacktriangleright y,[A,z]]

\displaystyle{}+[y,[x\blacktriangleright A,z]]+[y,[A,x\blacktriangleright z]]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle[x\blacktriangleright A,[y,z]]+[A,[x\blacktriangleright y,z]]+[A,[y,x\blacktriangleright z]]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle[x\blacktriangleright A,B]+[A,x\blacktriangleright B].

(v) Let
A=D_{z,w}
, then by (
8.2
), we get

\displaystyle A\blacktriangleright[x,y]=D_{z,w}\blacktriangleright D_{x,y}=D_{[z,w,x],y}+D_{x,[z,w,y]}=[A\blacktriangleright x,y]+[x,A\blacktriangleright y].

(vi) Let
A=D_{w,t}
and
B=D_{y,z}
, then

\displaystyle A\blacktriangleright[B,x]

\displaystyle=-[w,t,[y,z,x]]=-[[w,t,y],z,x]-[y,[w,t,z],x]-[y,z,[w,t,x]]

\displaystyle=[A\blacktriangleright B,x]+[B,A\blacktriangleright x].

(vii) From skew symmetry and the relation above gives

A\blacktriangleright[x,B]=-A\blacktriangleright[B,x]=-[A\blacktriangleright B,x]-[B,A\blacktriangleright x]=[A\blacktriangleright x,B]+[x,A\blacktriangleright B].

(viii)
A\blacktriangleright[B,C]=-[A,[B,C]]=-[[A,B],C]-[B,[A,C]]=[A\blacktriangleright B,C]+[B,A\blacktriangleright C]
.

(ix)
[x,y]\blacktriangleright z=D_{x,y}(z)=[x,y,z]
.

(x) From the definition of
(x\blacktriangleright A)(y)
, we have
(x\blacktriangleright A)(y)-x\blacktriangleright A(y)+A(x\blacktriangleright y)=0
, hence we get

\displaystyle[A,x]\blacktriangleright y

\displaystyle=-A(x)\blacktriangleright y+((x\blacktriangleright A)(y)-x\blacktriangleright A(y)+A(x\blacktriangleright y))

\displaystyle=-(A\blacktriangleright x)\blacktriangleright y+(x\blacktriangleright A)\blacktriangleright y-x\blacktriangleright(A\blacktriangleright y)+A\blacktriangleright(x\blacktriangleright y)=[A,x,y].

(xi) By skew symmetry and the relation above, we get

[x,A]\blacktriangleright y=-[A,x]\blacktriangleright y=-[A,x,y]=[x,A,y].

(xii) By the Jacobi identity, we have
[B,[A,x]]-[[B,A],x]-[A,[B,x]]=0
, hence

\displaystyle[A,B]\blacktriangleright x

\displaystyle=[A,B]\blacktriangleright x-([B,[A,x]]-[[B,A],x]-[A,[B,x]])

\displaystyle=[A,[B,x]]+[A,B]\blacktriangleright x-[B,[A,x]]+[[B,A],x]

\displaystyle=A\blacktriangleright(B\blacktriangleright x)-(A\blacktriangleright B)\blacktriangleright x-B\blacktriangleright(A\blacktriangleright x)+(B\blacktriangleright A)\blacktriangleright x=[A,B,x].

(xiii) Let
A=D_{z,w}
, then by repeatedly using (
8.1
), we get

\displaystyle[x,y,A]={}

\displaystyle x\blacktriangleright(y\blacktriangleright D_{z,w})-(x\blacktriangleright y)\blacktriangleright D_{z,w}-y\blacktriangleright(x\blacktriangleright D_{z,w})+(y\blacktriangleright x)\blacktriangleright D_{z,w}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle x\blacktriangleright(D_{y\blacktriangleright z,w}+D_{z,y\blacktriangleright w})-(D_{(x\blacktriangleright y)\blacktriangleright z,w}+D_{z,(x\blacktriangleright y)\blacktriangleright w})

\displaystyle{}-y\blacktriangleright(D_{x\blacktriangleright z,w}+D_{z,x\blacktriangleright w})+(D_{(y\blacktriangleright x)\blacktriangleright z,w}+D_{z,(y\blacktriangleright x)\blacktriangleright w})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle D_{x\blacktriangleright(y\blacktriangleright z),w}-D_{(x\blacktriangleright y)\blacktriangleright z,w}-D_{y\blacktriangleright(x\blacktriangleright z),w}+D_{(y\blacktriangleright x)\blacktriangleright z,w}

\displaystyle{}+D_{z,x\blacktriangleright(y\blacktriangleright w)}-D_{z,(x\blacktriangleright y)\blacktriangleright w}-D_{z,y\blacktriangleright(x\blacktriangleright w)}+D_{z,(y\blacktriangleright x)\blacktriangleright w}

\displaystyle{}+D_{y\blacktriangleright z,x\blacktriangleright w}+D_{x\blacktriangleright z,y\blacktriangleright w}-D_{x\blacktriangleright z,y\blacktriangleright w}-D_{y\blacktriangleright z,x\blacktriangleright w}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle D_{[x,y,z],w}+D_{z,[x,y,w]}.

By (
8.2
), we have

[x,y]\blacktriangleright A=D_{x,y}\blacktriangleright D_{z,w}=D_{[x,y,z],w}+D_{z,[x,y,w]}=[x,y,A].

(xiv) From (iv), we have
x\blacktriangleright[A,B]-[x\blacktriangleright A,B]-[A,x\blacktriangleright B]=0
, hence we get

\displaystyle[A,x]\blacktriangleright B

\displaystyle=[A,x]\blacktriangleright B+(x\blacktriangleright[A,B]-[x\blacktriangleright A,B]-[A,x\blacktriangleright B])

\displaystyle=-(A\blacktriangleright x)\blacktriangleright B-x\blacktriangleright(A\blacktriangleright B)+(x\blacktriangleright A)\blacktriangleright B+A\blacktriangleright(x\blacktriangleright B)=[A,x,B].

(xv) By skew symmetry and the relation above, we get

[x,A]\blacktriangleright B=-[A,x]\blacktriangleright B=-[A,x,B]=[x,A,B].

(xvi) The Jacobi identity gives
[B,[A,C]]-[[B,A],C]-[A,[B,C]]=0
, and we get

\displaystyle[A,B]\blacktriangleright C

\displaystyle{}=-[[A,B],C]-([B,[A,C]]-[[B,A],C]-[A,[B,C]])

\displaystyle{}=-(A\blacktriangleright B)\blacktriangleright C-B\blacktriangleright(A\blacktriangleright C)+(B\blacktriangleright A)\blacktriangleright C+A\blacktriangleright(B\blacktriangleright C)

\displaystyle{}=[A,B,C].

∎

Remark 8.7
.

There is a way to get a post-Lie structure associated to a homogeneous space by considering a sub-bundle of the tangent bundle of the frame bundle
[
12
]
. The construction of a post-Lie algebra on this bundle is very similar to the construction of a post-Lie algebra on
\operatorname{Der}(\mathcal{A})\oplus\mathcal{A}
presented in Theorem
8.6
.

For a Lie admissible triple algebra
\mathcal{A}
, let
\mathfrak{g}(\mathcal{A})
be the associated
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded post-Lie algebra of
\mathcal{A}
.

Corollary 8.8
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the basis of
\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})
given in Theorem
3.6
. Let
\hat{\mathcal{B}}
be defined by

(i)

if
x\in\mathcal{B}
then
x\in\hat{\mathcal{B}}
, and

(ii)

if
x,y\in\mathcal{B}
with
x>y
, then
[x,y]\in\hat{\mathcal{B}}
.

Then
\hat{\mathcal{B}}
is a basis for
\mathfrak{g}(\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C}))
.

Proof.

By Theorem
8.6
,
\mathfrak{g}(\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C}))=\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})\oplus\operatorname{Der}(\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C}))
where
\operatorname{Der}(\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C}))=\{[x,y]\mid x,y\in\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})\}
. By Theorem
3.6
, we know that
\mathcal{B}
is a basis for
\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C})
, and by definition of
\operatorname{Der}(\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C}))
we get that
\{[x,y]\mid x,y\in\mathcal{B},\,x>y\}
is a basis of
\operatorname{Der}(\operatorname{LAT}(\mathcal{C}))
.
∎

Appendix A
Appendix

Lemma A.1
.

For all positive integers
n
and
k
, the following relation holds:

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(n-j)\binom{(k-2)+(n-j)}{(n-j)}+\binom{k+(n-1)}{(n-1)}=n\binom{(k-1)+(n-1)}{(n-1)}.

(A.1)

Proof.

Let
\beta_{k}(n)
be given as the left-hand side of (
A.1
). Notice that this equation is trivially true for all
k
when
n=1
, and assume that is true for all
k
when
n<N
for some integer
N
. Notice that

\displaystyle\beta_{k}(N-1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N-2}(N-1-j)\binom{(k-2)+(N-1-j)}{(N-1-j)}+\binom{k+(N-2)}{(N-2)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{N-1}(N-j)\binom{(k-2)+(N-j)}{(N-j)}+\binom{k+(N-2)}{(N-2)}

\displaystyle=\beta_{k}(N)-(N-1)\binom{(k-2)+(N-1)}{(N-1)}-\binom{k+(N-1)}{(N-1)}+\binom{k+(N-2)}{(N-2)}.

Rewriting this equality and using the assumption, we get

\beta_{k}(N)=(N-1)\binom{k+N-3}{N-2}+(N-1)\binom{k+N-3}{N-1}+\binom{k+N-1}{N-1}-\binom{k+N-2}{N-2}.

Using Pascal’s rule,
\binom{m}{r}=\binom{m-1}{r}+\binom{m-1}{r-1}
, we get

\beta_{k}(N)=(N-1)\binom{k+N-2}{N-1}+\binom{k+N-2}{N-1}=N\binom{(k-1)+(N-1)}{(N-1)}

and the lemma follows by induction on
n
.
∎

Appendix B
Appendix

Recall

\displaystyle x\rhd(uv)=(x\rhd u)v+u(x\rhd v),

\displaystyle(xu)\rhd v=x\rhd(u\rhd v)-(x\rhd u)\rhd v

for
x\in\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
and
u,v\in D(\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C}))
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
x_{0},\dots,x_{n},r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}\in\operatorname{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
. Then

\displaystyle(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\in\mathcal{I},

where
I
,
J
,
K
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
I\cup J\cup K=\{1,\dots,n\}
, and for any
s<t
we have
i_{s}<i_{t}
,
j_{s}<j_{t}
and
k_{s}<k_{t}
.

Proof.

If
n=1
, this is true by definition of
\mathcal{I}
. Assume it is true for
n=k
, then we get

\displaystyle(x_{0}x_{1}\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]=x_{0}\rhd((x_{1}\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}])

\displaystyle\hphantom{(x_0 x_1 \cdots x_k) \rhd[r_1, r_2, r_3] =}{}-\sum_{s=1}^{k}(x_{1}\cdots(x_{0}\rhd x_{s})\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}],

and we know that

\displaystyle(x_{1}\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\in\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle(x_{1}\cdots(x_{0}\rhd x_{s})\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\bigl(\tilde{x}_{i_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(\tilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(\tilde{x}_{k_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\in\mathcal{I},

where
\tilde{x}_{t}=x_{t}
whenever
t\neq s
and
\tilde{x}_{s}=x_{0}\rhd x_{s}
. We also have

\displaystyle\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl(x_{0}\rhd[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl(}{}-\bigl[x_{0}\rhd\bigl(\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1}\bigr),\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]

\displaystyle\hphantom{\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl(}{}-\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},x_{0}\rhd\bigl(\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2}\bigr),\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]

\displaystyle\hphantom{\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl(}{}-\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},x_{0}\rhd\bigl(\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)\in\mathcal{I}.

Notice that

x_{0}\rhd\bigl(\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1}\bigr)=\bigl(x_{0}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1}+\sum_{s=i_{1}}^{i_{|I|}}\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots(x_{0}\rhd x_{s})\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},

hence we can rewrite the relation above as

\displaystyle\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl(x_{0}\rhd\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{s=1}^{k}\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\bigl(\tilde{x}_{i_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(\tilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(\tilde{x}_{k_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\hat{I},\hat{J},\hat{K}}\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|\hat{I}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|\hat{J}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|\hat{K}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\in\mathcal{I},

where
\hat{I}
,
\hat{J}
and
\hat{K}
are defined in the same way as
I
,
J
and
K
, just on the set
\{0,1,\dots,k\}
. When put together, this gives

\displaystyle x_{0}\rhd\bigl((x_{1}\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{s=1}^{k}\biggl((x_{1}\cdots(x_{0}\rhd x_{s})\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad{}\hphantom{-\sum_{s=1}^{k}\biggl(}{}-\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\bigl(\tilde{x}_{i_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(\tilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(\tilde{x}_{k_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl(x_{0}\rhd\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{s=1}^{k}\sum_{I,J,K}\bigl[\bigl(\tilde{x}_{i_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{i_{|I|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(\tilde{x}_{j_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{j_{|J|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(\tilde{x}_{k_{1}}\cdots\tilde{x}_{k_{|K|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{\hat{I},\hat{J},\hat{K}}\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|\hat{I}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|\hat{J}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|\hat{K}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]

\displaystyle\quad{}=x_{0}\rhd((x_{1}\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}])-\sum_{s=1}^{k}(x_{1}\cdots(x_{0}\rhd x_{s})\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{\hat{I},\hat{J},\hat{K}}\bigl[\bigl(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|\hat{I}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|\hat{J}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|\hat{K}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]

\displaystyle\quad{}=(x_{0}x_{1}\cdots x_{k})\rhd[r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}]

\displaystyle\qquad{}-\sum_{\hat{I},\hat{J},\hat{K}}\bigl[\bigr(x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{|\hat{I}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{1},\bigl(x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{|\hat{J}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{2},\bigl(x_{k_{1}}\cdots x_{k_{|\hat{K}|}}\bigr)\rhd r_{3}\bigr]\in\mathcal{I}.

Hence the statement is true for
n=k+1
, and the lemma follows by induction.
∎
